Yeah, I guess I have. I mean, after my shifts, I used to always do something to decompress, you know? I would paint, or go to yoga, or even take salsa classes. I used to really love salsa. Sleep? Um, no, not really. I mean, I think I'm sleeping okay. I usually go to bed around the same time, but honestly, the mattress we have is starting to get this weird dip on my side. My coworker, Sarah, tried to bring in her own French press last month, but then she accidentally knocked it off the breakroom table and glass went everywhere. It took us like forty-five minutes to clean it up because our custodian, Gary, was on vacation in Florida. He always goes to this same spot in Boca Raton where his sister has a condo, and he brings back these really tacky seashell magnets for everyone. I have like three of them on my fridge right now, next to this picture of my cat... but yeah, I just don't really have an appetite. As for feeling guilty, I don't know, I just feel like I'm doing everything wrong right now.

Yeah, I mean... that's kind of why I made the appointment. For the past few weeks, I've just felt this heavy, sad pressure sitting on me almost all day. It's like I'm dragging around wet sand. Just yesterday, I was in with Mr. Henderson—he's this sweet older man on my floor who always asks about my cat, Barnaby. Barnaby actually just had to get switched to this expensive prescription diet because he was throwing up on my rugs, which is a whole other nightmare trying to get him to actually eat the new kibble... but anyway, I'm there at work, smiling at Mr. Henderson and the other patients, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any loss of interest or pleasure in activities you usually enjoy, like spending time with your cat or anything else that normally brings you joy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. I mean, after my shifts, I used to always do something to decompress, you know? I would paint, or go to yoga, or even take salsa classes. I used to really love salsa. My friend Jenna dragged me to this studio downtown a couple years ago, and the instructor there was so intense, always clapping and yelling about our footwork. Jenna actually just twisted her ankle doing Zumba last week, so she's stuck in this massive walking boot, which is a total nightmare for her because she lives in a third-floor walk-up and her dog refuses to go down the stairs by himself... but anyway, even before she hurt her ankle, I had completely stopped going. \n\nLately I just go straight home, sit on the couch, and that's it. Jenna will text me, or my mom will text me, and I just ignore all of them. I look at the phone and just put it face down because none of it even feels worth the effort anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the sadness and loss of interest in activities?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been about three weeks now. It started right after I found out I was pregnant, which... you know, was a huge shock. I wasn't planning on it, and my boyfriend, Mark, he's currently out of town on this massive contracting job in Ohio. He builds these custom decks, like the really fancy ones with the built-in seating and the pergolas. He's been sending me pictures of the wood grain, which is nice, I guess, but it's just so loud on the construction site he can barely hear me when I call. And his truck just broke down last week, so he's been having to borrow his boss's van, which smells entirely like wet dog and stale French fries... but yeah, anyway, it's been about three weeks since I found out, and that's when all this really started feeling so heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately, like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than usual?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Um, no, not really. I mean, I think I'm sleeping okay. I usually go to bed around the same time, but honestly, the mattress we have is starting to get this weird dip on my side. Mark says it's because I always sleep curled up in the exact same spot, but I swear it's just a cheap mattress. We bought it online from one of those places that ships it to you in a tiny box, and you have to cut the plastic and it just... whoosh, inflates. It was terrifying, actually, I thought it was going to knock over my nightstand. My nightstand is this antique thing I found at a flea market, real heavy oak, but the bottom drawer always sticks if it gets too humid. And lately, the humidity has been awful, hasn't it? Just walking to my car after my shift, I feel like I'm swimming through the air. But anyway, yeah, I don't think my sleep has really changed much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately, or found yourself feeling excessively guilty or worthless?\n\nPatient: I haven't really been eating much, honestly. I know I should be eating regularly, especially now with the pregnancy and everything, but most days food just sounds completely unappealing. Like, I'll be halfway through a shift at the clinic and suddenly realize all I've had all day is some crackers and coffee. The coffee at the clinic is terrible, by the way. They buy these massive tubs of the cheapest grounds they can find, and the machine hasn't been descaled since I started working there. My coworker, Sarah, tried to bring in her own French press last month, but then she accidentally knocked it off the breakroom table and glass went everywhere. It took us like forty-five minutes to clean it up because our custodian, Gary, was on vacation in Florida. He always goes to this same spot in Boca Raton where his sister has a condo, and he brings back these really tacky seashell magnets for everyone. I have like three of them on my fridge right now, next to this picture of my cat... but yeah, I just don't really have an appetite. As for feeling guilty, I don't know, I just feel like I'm doing everything wrong right now.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, or have you experienced a lack of interest or pleasure in most activities most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... that's kind of why I made the appointment. One of my patients yesterday, Mr. Henderson, he brought in these fresh tomatoes from his garden. Huge, beautiful heirlooms. He's so sweet, he always wears these suspenders with little trout on them because he used to be a fly fisherman. He told me all about how you have to tie the flies yourself to really get the big ones, and he uses this special kind of feather... I can't remember what bird it's from, maybe a pheasant? Anyway, he was so proud of those tomatoes, and I just stood there smiling and thanking him, but all I wanted to do was go hide in the supply closet and cry. I ended up leaving the tomatoes on the breakroom counter because I knew I wouldn't eat them, and I think Sarah took them home to make salsa. She makes this really spicy mango salsa that she brings to our summer potlucks, but she always uses way too much cilantro. I have that gene, you know, where cilantro tastes exactly like dish soap? It completely ruins tacos for me. Yeah, it really has. I'd say it's been going on for about three weeks now. It started shortly after I found out I was pregnant. Which, honestly, finding out was a whole thing in itself. I was at the drugstore buying those little travel-sized toothpastes because Mark and I were supposed to go up to his parents' cabin for the weekend. His parents have this gorgeous place right on the lake, but the water pressure in the guest bathroom is an absolute joke. You turn on the shower and it's just this sad little trickle. Mark's dad keeps saying he's going to fix the pipes, but he's been saying that since we started dating five years ago. He's always starting these DIY projects and never finishing them. Like last Thanksgiving, he decided to build a birdhouse out of reclaimed wood from an old barn, and it's still just sitting in pieces on his workbench. But anyway, I was in the toothpaste aisle and I just had this sudden feeling, so I grabbed a test on a whim. And yeah, since right around then, it's just been this constant, heavy feeling every single day.

I'd say it's been going on for about three weeks now. It started shortly after I found out I was pregnant. Which, honestly, finding out was a whole thing in itself. I was at the drugstore buying those little travel-sized toothpastes because Mark and I were supposed to go up to his parents' cabin for the weekend. His parents have this gorgeous place right on the lake, but the water pressure in the guest bathroom is an absolute joke. You turn on the shower and it's just this sad little trickle. Mark's dad keeps saying he's going to fix the pipes, but he's been saying that since we started dating five years ago. He's always starting these DIY projects and never finishing them. Like last Thanksgiving, he decided to build a birdhouse out of reclaimed wood from an old barn, and it's still just sitting in pieces on his workbench. But anyway, I was in the toothpaste aisle and I just had this sudden feeling, so I grabbed a test on a whim. And yeah, since right around then, it's just been this constant, heavy feeling every single day.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, have you felt restless or been unable to sit still?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 28 / licensed_practical_or_licensed_vocational_nurse / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Psychomotor_Changes\nmanifestation: \"Everything I do feels slowed down, like I’m moving through syrup, and even simple things at the clinic like gathering wound supplies or finishing charting take me noticeably longer than they should.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I definitely have. Everything I do just feels so slowed down right now. It's honestly like I'm moving through syrup or something. Even doing really simple things at the clinic, like gathering wound care supplies or finishing up my charting, it's taking me noticeably longer than it should. Which is so frustrating because our charting system is already a nightmare. They updated the software last month to this new program called HealthSync, and it's supposed to be more "streamlined," but it actually takes like five extra clicks just to enter a patient's temperature. And the IT guy, Kevin, came in to give us a tutorial on it, but he spent the first twenty minutes just complaining about his commute. He drives in from the suburbs and apparently there's this one intersection where the light takes a full four minutes to change. He actually timed it on his phone. He showed me the stopwatch app and everything. He has this really weird phone case, too, it looks like a giant silicone waffle. I asked him where he got it and he said his niece bought it for him at a kiosk in the mall. I haven't been to the mall in ages, mostly because the parking lot is a disaster since they started building that new movie theater. But yeah, I just feel really sluggish and slow doing everything.

They updated the software last month to this new program called HealthSync, and it's supposed to be more \"streamlined,\" but it actually takes like five extra clicks just to enter a patient's temperature. And the IT guy, Kevin, came in to give us a tutorial on it, but he spent the first twenty minutes just complaining about his commute. He drives in from the suburbs and apparently there's this one intersection where the light takes a full four minutes to change. He actually timed it on his phone. I asked him where he got it and he said his niece bought it for him at a kiosk in the mall. I haven't been to the mall in ages, mostly because the parking lot is a disaster since they started building that new movie theater. I don't think so, no. I haven't really noticed feeling physically tired or like I don't have energy. I mean, my energy levels are pretty much the same as always. She drives a Honda Civic, one of the older ones, and the AC is completely shot. She took it to a mechanic down there and he quoted her like, eight hundred dollars to fix it. So she's just been driving with the windows down, which messes up her hair. She has this really curly hair, kind of like our mom's, and the humidity just makes it poof out. But um, anyway, no, I haven't felt restless or anything like that. Just really slow, like I said before.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had periods in your life where you felt down or lost interest in things for most of the day, more days than not, for as long as two years?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 28 / licensed_practical_or_licensed_vocational_nurse / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't think I've ever felt like that for that long. Two years is a really long time. I mean, I've had times where I felt down, but definitely not for years at a time. The longest I've ever felt kind of stuck in a rut was maybe a few months back when I was in nursing school. That was brutal. The clinical rotations were just completely exhausting. I had this one instructor, Mrs. Gable, and she was absolutely terrifying. She used to wear these really loud, clunky clogs, so you could always hear her coming down the hallway. It sounded like a horse trotting on linoleum. We used to call her the Clydesdale, but obviously never to her face. She was obsessed with making sure our uniform scrubs were perfectly ironed. Like, who irons scrubs? They're supposed to be wash-and-wear. I bought this cheap iron from a thrift store just to appease her, but it had this weird short in the cord, so if you wiggled it the wrong way, it would spark. I actually scorched a hole right through the pocket of my favorite top. I had to sew a patch over it, and I used this thread that was slightly the wrong shade of blue, and Mrs. Gable totally noticed. She gave me a whole lecture about professional appearance right in front of a patient who was eating jello. It was cherry jello, which is the worst flavor, in my opinion. But yeah, anyway, no, I haven't felt down or lost interest in things for two whole years. That sounds awful.

Barnaby actually just had to get switched to this expensive prescription diet because he was throwing up on my rugs, which is a whole other nightmare trying to get him to actually eat the new kibble... but anyway, I'm there at work, smiling at Mr. Henderson and the other patients, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any loss of interest or pleasure in activities you usually enjoy, like spending time with your cat or anything else that normally brings you joy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. I mean, after my shifts, I used to always do something to decompress, you know? I would paint, or go to yoga, or even take salsa classes. I used to really love salsa. Jenna actually just twisted her ankle doing Zumba last week, so she's stuck in this massive walking boot, which is a total nightmare for her because she lives in a third-floor walk-up and her dog refuses to go down the stairs by himself... but anyway, even before she hurt her ankle, I had completely stopped going. \n\nLately I just go straight home, sit on the couch, and that's it. Jenna will text me, or my mom will text me, and I just ignore all of them. Um, no, not really. I mean, I think I'm sleeping okay. I usually go to bed around the same time, but honestly, the mattress we have is starting to get this weird dip on my side. Mark says it's because I always sleep curled up in the exact same spot, but I swear it's just a cheap mattress. We bought it online from one of those places that ships it to you in a tiny box, and you have to cut the plastic and it just... whoosh, inflates. It was terrifying, actually, I thought it was going to knock over my nightstand. My nightstand is this antique thing I found at a flea market, real heavy oak, but the bottom drawer always sticks if it gets too humid. And lately, the humidity has been awful, hasn't it? Just walking to my car after my shift, I feel like I'm swimming through the air. But anyway, yeah, I don't think my sleep has really changed much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately, or found yourself feeling excessively guilty or worthless?\n\nPatient: I haven't really been eating much, honestly. I know I should be eating regularly, especially now with the pregnancy and everything, but most days food just sounds completely unappealing. Like, I'll be halfway through a shift at the clinic and suddenly realize all I've had all day is some crackers and coffee. The coffee at the clinic is terrible, by the way. They buy these massive tubs of the cheapest grounds they can find, and the machine hasn't been descaled since I started working there. My coworker, Sarah, tried to bring in her own French press last month, but then she accidentally knocked it off the breakroom table and glass went everywhere. It took us like forty-five minutes to clean it up because our custodian, Gary, was on vacation in Florida. He always goes to this same spot in Boca Raton where his sister has a condo, and he brings back these really tacky seashell magnets for everyone. I have like three of them on my fridge right now, next to this picture of my cat... but yeah, I just don't really have an appetite. As for feeling guilty, I don't know, I just feel like I'm doing everything wrong right now.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, or have you experienced a lack of interest or pleasure in most activities most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... that's kind of why I made the appointment. For the past few weeks, I've just felt this heavy, sad pressure sitting on me almost all day. It's like I'm dragging around wet sand. And it's so hard because at the clinic, I have to be the cheerful nurse, you know? I'm taking blood pressures and smiling at the patients, asking about their grandchildren, but inside I feel like I'm barely holding myself together. One of my patients yesterday, Mr. Henderson, he brought in these fresh tomatoes from his garden. Huge, beautiful heirlooms. He's so sweet, he always wears these suspenders with little trout on them because he used to be a fly fisherman. He told me all about how you have to tie the flies yourself to really get the big ones, and he uses this special kind of feather... I can't remember what bird it's from, maybe a pheasant? Anyway, he was so proud of those tomatoes, and I just stood there smiling and thanking him, but all I wanted to do was go hide in the supply closet and cry. I ended up leaving the tomatoes on the breakroom counter because I knew I wouldn't eat them, and I think Sarah took them home to make salsa. She makes this really spicy mango salsa that she brings to our summer potlucks, but she always uses way too much cilantro. I have that gene, you know, where cilantro tastes exactly like dish soap? It completely ruins tacos for me. But yeah, nearly every day it's just been this overwhelming, hopeless feeling that I can't shake.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood or lack of interest in things has been present most of the day, nearly every day, for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really has. I'd say it's been going on for about three weeks now. It started shortly after I found out I was pregnant. Which, honestly, finding out was a whole thing in itself. I was at the drugstore buying those little travel-sized toothpastes because Mark and I were supposed to go up to his parents' cabin for the weekend. His parents have this gorgeous place right on the lake, but the water pressure in the guest bathroom is an absolute joke. You turn on the shower and it's just this sad little trickle. Mark's dad keeps saying he's going to fix the pipes, but he's been saying that since we started dating five years ago. He's always starting these DIY projects and never finishing them. Like last Thanksgiving, he decided to build a birdhouse out of reclaimed wood from an old barn, and it's still just sitting in pieces on his workbench. But anyway, I was in the toothpaste aisle and I just had this sudden feeling, so I grabbed a test on a whim. And yeah, since right around then, it's just been this constant, heavy feeling every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, have you felt restless or been unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely have. Everything I do just feels so slowed down right now. It's honestly like I'm moving through syrup or something. Even doing really simple things at the clinic, like gathering wound care supplies or finishing up my charting, it's taking me noticeably longer than it should. Which is so frustrating because our charting system is already a nightmare. They updated the software last month to this new program called HealthSync, and it's supposed to be more \"streamlined,\" but it actually takes like five extra clicks just to enter a patient's temperature. And the IT guy, Kevin, came in to give us a tutorial on it, but he spent the first twenty minutes just complaining about his commute. He drives in from the suburbs and apparently there's this one intersection where the light takes a full four minutes to change. He actually timed it on his phone. He showed me the stopwatch app and everything. He has this really weird phone case, too, it looks like a giant silicone waffle. I asked him where he got it and he said his niece bought it for him at a kiosk in the mall. I haven't been to the mall in ages, mostly because the parking lot is a disaster since they started building that new movie theater. Dr. Evans tried to descale it last week using straight white vinegar, and he definitely didn't run enough water through it afterward to rinse it out. The entire back hallway smelled exactly like a pickle factory for three days. It was so gross. So they keep exploding, and now there's just crusted coffee grounds permanently jammed up in the little needle part. I'm taking blood pressures and smiling at the patients, asking about their grandchildren, but inside I feel like I'm barely holding myself together. One of my patients yesterday, Mr. Henderson, he brought in these fresh tomatoes from his garden. Huge, beautiful heirlooms. He's so sweet, he always wears these suspenders with little trout on them because he used to be a fly fisherman. Anyway, he was so proud of those tomatoes, and I just stood there smiling and thanking him, but all I wanted to do was go hide in the supply closet and cry. I ended up leaving the tomatoes on the breakroom counter because I knew I wouldn't eat them, and I think Sarah took them home to make salsa. She makes this really spicy mango salsa that she brings to our summer potlucks, but she always uses way too much cilantro. I have that gene, you know, where cilantro tastes exactly like dish soap? It completely ruins tacos for me. I'd say it's been going on for about three weeks now. It started shortly after I found out I was pregnant. Which, honestly, finding out was a whole thing in itself. I was at the drugstore buying those little travel-sized toothpastes because Mark and I were supposed to go up to his parents' cabin for the weekend. His parents have this gorgeous place right on the lake, but the water pressure in the guest bathroom is an absolute joke. You turn on the shower and it's just this sad little trickle. Mark's dad keeps saying he's going to fix the pipes, but he's been saying that since we started dating five years ago. He's always starting these DIY projects and never finishing them. Like last Thanksgiving, he decided to build a birdhouse out of reclaimed wood from an old barn, and it's still just sitting in pieces on his workbench. But anyway, I was in the toothpaste aisle and I just had this sudden feeling, so I grabbed a test on a whim. And yeah, since right around then, it's just been this constant, heavy feeling every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, have you felt restless or been unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely have. Everything I do just feels so slowed down right now. It's honestly like I'm moving through syrup or something. Even doing really simple things at the clinic, like gathering wound care supplies or finishing up my charting, it's taking me noticeably longer than it should. Which is so frustrating because our charting system is already a nightmare. They updated the software last month to this new program called HealthSync, and it's supposed to be more \"streamlined,\" but it actually takes like five extra clicks just to enter a patient's temperature. And the IT guy, Kevin, came in to give us a tutorial on it, but he spent the first twenty minutes just complaining about his commute. He drives in from the suburbs and apparently there's this one intersection where the light takes a full four minutes to change. He actually timed it on his phone. He showed me the stopwatch app and everything. He has this really weird phone case, too, it looks like a giant silicone waffle. Jenna will text me, or my mom will text me, and I just ignore all of them. I look at the phone and just put it face down because none of it even feels worth the effort anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the sadness and loss of interest in activities?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been about three weeks now. It started right after I found out I was pregnant, which... you know, was a huge shock. I wasn't planning on it, and my boyfriend, Mark, he's currently out of town on this massive contracting job in Ohio. He builds these custom decks, like the really fancy ones with the built-in seating and the pergolas. He's been sending me pictures of the wood grain, which is nice, I guess, but it's just so loud on the construction site he can barely hear me when I call. And his truck just broke down last week, so he's been having to borrow his boss's van, which smells entirely like wet dog and stale French fries... but yeah, anyway, it's been about three weeks since I found out, and that's when all this really started feeling so heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately, like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than usual?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Um, no, not really. I mean, I think I'm sleeping okay. I usually go to bed around the same time, but honestly, the mattress we have is starting to get this weird dip on my side. Mark says it's because I always sleep curled up in the exact same spot, but I swear it's just a cheap mattress. We bought it online from one of those places that ships it to you in a tiny box, and you have to cut the plastic and it just... whoosh, inflates. It was terrifying, actually, I thought it was going to knock over my nightstand. My nightstand is this antique thing I found at a flea market, real heavy oak, but the bottom drawer always sticks if it gets too humid. And lately, the humidity has been awful, hasn't it? Just walking to my car after my shift, I feel like I'm swimming through the air. But anyway, yeah, I don't think my sleep has really changed much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately, or found yourself feeling excessively guilty or worthless?\n\nPatient: I haven't really been eating much, honestly. I know I should be eating regularly, especially now with the pregnancy and everything, but most days food just sounds completely unappealing. Like, I'll be halfway through a shift at the clinic and suddenly realize all I've had all day is some crackers and coffee. The coffee at the clinic is terrible, by the way. They buy these massive tubs of the cheapest grounds they can find, and the machine hasn't been descaled since I started working there. My coworker, Sarah, tried to bring in her own French press last month, but then she accidentally knocked it off the breakroom table and glass went everywhere. It took us like forty-five minutes to clean it up because our custodian, Gary, was on vacation in Florida. He always goes to this same spot in Boca Raton where his sister has a condo, and he brings back these really tacky seashell magnets for everyone. I have like three of them on my fridge right now, next to this picture of my cat... but yeah, I just don't really have an appetite. As for feeling guilty, I don't know, I just feel like I'm doing everything wrong right now.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, or have you experienced a lack of interest or pleasure in most activities most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... that's kind of why I made the appointment. For the past few weeks, I've just felt this heavy, sad pressure sitting on me almost all day. It's like I'm dragging around wet sand. And it's so hard because at the clinic, I have to be the cheerful nurse, you know? I'm taking blood pressures and smiling at the patients, asking about their grandchildren, but inside I feel like I'm barely holding myself together. One of my patients yesterday, Mr. Henderson, he brought in these fresh tomatoes from his garden. Huge, beautiful heirlooms. He's so sweet, he always wears these suspenders with little trout on them because he used to be a fly fisherman. He told me all about how you have to tie the flies yourself to really get the big ones, and he uses this special kind of feather... I can't remember what bird it's from, maybe a pheasant? Anyway, he was so proud of those tomatoes, and I just stood there smiling and thanking him, but all I wanted to do was go hide in the supply closet and cry. I ended up leaving the tomatoes on the breakroom counter because I knew I wouldn't eat them, and I think Sarah took them home to make salsa. She makes this really spicy mango salsa that she brings to our summer potlucks, but she always uses way too much cilantro. I have that gene, you know, where cilantro tastes exactly like dish soap? It completely ruins tacos for me. But yeah, nearly every day it's just been this overwhelming, hopeless feeling that I can't shake.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood or lack of interest in things has been present most of the day, nearly every day, for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really has. I'd say it's been going on for about three weeks now. It started shortly after I found out I was pregnant. Which, honestly, finding out was a whole thing in itself. I was at the drugstore buying those little travel-sized toothpastes because Mark and I were supposed to go up to his parents' cabin for the weekend. His parents have this gorgeous place right on the lake, but the water pressure in the guest bathroom is an absolute joke. You turn on the shower and it's just this sad little trickle. Mark's dad keeps saying he's going to fix the pipes, but he's been saying that since we started dating five years ago. He's always starting these DIY projects and never finishing them. Like last Thanksgiving, he decided to build a birdhouse out of reclaimed wood from an old barn, and it's still just sitting in pieces on his workbench. But anyway, I was in the toothpaste aisle and I just had this sudden feeling, so I grabbed a test on a whim. And yeah, since right around then, it's just been this constant, heavy feeling every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, have you felt restless or been unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely have. Everything I do just feels so slowed down right now. It's honestly like I'm moving through syrup or something. Even doing really simple things at the clinic, like gathering wound care supplies or finishing up my charting, it's taking me noticeably longer than it should. Which is so frustrating because our charting system is already a nightmare. They updated the software last month to this new program called HealthSync, and it's supposed to be more \"streamlined,\" but it actually takes like five extra clicks just to enter a patient's temperature. And the IT guy, Kevin, came in to give us a tutorial on it, but he spent the first twenty minutes just complaining about his commute. He drives in from the suburbs and apparently there's this one intersection where the light takes a full four minutes to change. He actually timed it on his phone. He showed me the stopwatch app and everything. He has this really weird phone case, too, it looks like a giant silicone waffle. I asked him where he got it and he said his niece bought it for him at a kiosk in the mall. I haven't been to the mall in ages, mostly because the parking lot is a disaster since they started building that new movie theater. But yeah, I just feel really sluggish and slow doing everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt fatigued or noticed a loss of energy almost every day for the past two weeks, even when you haven't been physically active?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I haven't really noticed feeling physically tired or like I don't have energy. I mean, my energy levels are pretty much the same as always. I still drink my morning coffee, but that's just my normal routine. Though I might have to start stopping at a drive-thru before work because the Keurig in the clinic breakroom is completely ruined right now. Dr. Evans tried to descale it last week using straight white vinegar, and he definitely didn't run enough water through it afterward to rinse it out. The entire back hallway smelled exactly like a pickle factory for three days. It was so gross. And he always buys those really cheap off-brand pods from the bulk store, the ones where the foil lid is super flimsy? So they keep exploding, and now there's just crusted coffee grounds permanently jammed up in the little needle part. I actually tried to clean it out yesterday with a paperclip, but I slipped and poked my thumb. I had to go dig a Band-Aid out of the pediatric supply bin, so I've been walking around taking adult patients' vitals with a giant SpongeBob SquarePants on my hand. The kids love them, obviously, but Mr. Henderson—the tomato guy—he actually asked me if SpongeBob was a type of Swiss cheese. But yeah, um... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, fatigue. No, I wouldn't say I've been fatigued or lacking energy, I just feel really slow doing things, like I mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so restless inside that you couldn't sit still, or found yourself pacing, fidgeting, or moving around more than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt restless like that. I'm not really pacing or fidgeting or anything. Honestly, I spend most of my time sitting at the nurses' station when I'm not in with a patient. The chairs there are awful, by the way. They're those rolling office chairs from like, the 1990s, and the fabric on mine is this weird, scratchy maroon color. Someone spilled hand sanitizer on it last year and it left this giant bleached stain that looks exactly like a map of Florida. Which is funny, because my sister just moved to Florida. She got a job in Orlando, working in the HR department for this company that makes pool noodles. I didn't even know there was a whole corporate structure for pool noodles, but apparently there is. She says it's so hot there that she has to keep her steering wheel covered with a towel when she parks at the grocery store. She drives a Honda Civic, one of the older ones, and the AC is completely shot. She took it to a mechanic down there and he quoted her like, eight hundred dollars to fix it. So she's just been driving with the windows down, which messes up her hair. She has this really curly hair, kind of like our mom's, and the humidity just makes it poof out. But um, anyway, no, I haven't felt restless or anything like that. Just really slow, like I said before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods in your life where you felt down or lost interest in things for most of the day, more days than not, for as long as two years?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I've ever felt like that for that long. Two years is a really long time. I mean, I've had times where I felt down, but definitely not for years at a time. The longest I've ever felt kind of stuck in a rut was maybe a few months back when I was in nursing school. That was brutal. The clinical rotations were just completely exhausting. I had this one instructor, Mrs. Gable, and she was absolutely terrifying. She used to wear these really loud, clunky clogs, so you could always hear her coming down the hallway. It sounded like a horse trotting on linoleum. We used to call her the Clydesdale, but obviously never to her face. She was obsessed with making sure our uniform scrubs were perfectly ironed. Like, who irons scrubs? They're supposed to be wash-and-wear. I bought this cheap iron from a thrift store just to appease her, but it had this weird short in the cord, so if you wiggled it the wrong way, it would spark. I actually scorched a hole right through the pocket of my favorite top. I had to sew a patch over it, and I used this thread that was slightly the wrong shade of blue, and Mrs. Gable totally noticed. She gave me a whole lecture about professional appearance right in front of a patient who was eating jello. It was cherry jello, which is the worst flavor, in my opinion. But yeah, anyway, no, I haven't felt down or lost interest in things for two whole years. That sounds awful.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or lost interest in most things, more days than not, before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I've never really had a time like that before this started, definitely not for two weeks straight. I mean, I'm usually a pretty upbeat person. Like, even when things get crazy at the clinic. Just last week, we had this massive delivery of medical supplies, and the delivery guy—he's this new guy, really young, maybe early twenties—he stacked all the boxes right in front of the supply closet door. Like, completely blocked it. And of course, that was the exact moment Dr. Evans decided he urgently needed a specific type of tongue depressor. The flavored ones, you know? We have grape and cherry for the kids, but he specifically wanted the grape ones because he says they taste less like cough syrup. Anyway, I had to move like fifteen boxes of gauze and saline solution just to get the door open. And the boxes were so heavy! I don't even know why we ordered that much saline. We barely go through a fraction of that in a month. I think the office manager, Brenda, might have accidentally added an extra zero to the order form. She's been a little distracted lately because her daughter is getting married in the fall, and they're having all this drama over the floral arrangements. Apparently, hydrangeas are out of season or something? I don't know much about flowers, honestly. I tried to keep a succulent alive on my desk once, but I think I overwatered it because it just turned to mush. It was really sad. But, um... yeah, to answer your question, no, I've never felt this way for a couple of weeks before all this current stuff started.\n\nDoctor: Before this current episode, have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or lost interest in most things, more days than not?\n\nPatient: Wait, didn't you just ask me that? Or maybe I'm just getting confused, sorry. But no, I haven't ever had anything like this happen before. This is the first time I've felt this way. \n\nHonestly, repeating things is basically my whole day at the clinic right now anyway. Like, our fax machine has been stuck in this weird loop since Monday. It keeps printing out the exact same lunch menu from this Thai restaurant down the street. We didn't even order from there! Though, honestly, I love Pad Thai. There's this place near my apartment that makes it perfectly spicy, but they always skimp on the crushed peanuts. I don't get why they're so stingy with the peanuts, they buy them in bulk. I actually bought a giant jar of dry-roasted peanuts from Costco just to keep in my pantry so I can add my own when I get takeout. I got the honey-roasted ones by mistake last time, which sounds kind of gross to put in noodles, but it actually wasn't terrible. It gave it this whole sweet and savory vibe. I tried to tell Brenda about it—the office manager I mentioned—but she was on the phone arguing with the caterer about whether or not they should serve mini quiches at the wedding reception. Mini quiches always get cold too fast, if you ask me. \n\nBut anyway, sorry, what were we saying? Oh, right. No, I've never had a two-week period of feeling down like this before. It's all totally new.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I feel like we're going in circles, Doc. Like I said, no. This is definitely the first time I've ever felt this... this bad for this long. Two weeks, two years, whatever—it's never happened before. 

It's funny you keep asking the same thing, though, because it reminds me of my cat, Barnaby. He's this massive orange tabby, and he has this routine every morning where he paws at the same spot on the bedroom door until I get up to feed him. It's not even a scratch, it's just this dull *thump, thump, thump*. And if I ignore him, he doesn't stop. He just waits exactly three minutes and does it again. I actually timed him once. I bought this little digital stopwatch because I thought it was so weird. The stopwatch was neon green, which I loved, but I accidentally left it in the pocket of my scrub pants and it went through the wash. Totally ruined it. Which is a shame, because I was going to use it to time how long it takes Dr. Evans to dictate his notes. He talks so slow, it's agonizing. He pauses between every other word like he's waiting for profound inspiration to strike. I swear, I could knit a whole sweater in the time it takes him to finish a single patient chart. I tried knitting once, actually, but I kept dropping stitches, so I ended up with this weird, lumpy triangle instead of a scarf. 

But yeah, no. Never had this happen before. This is all completely new territory for me.

I was at the drugstore buying those little travel-sized toothpastes because Mark and I were supposed to go up to his parents' cabin for the weekend. His parents have this gorgeous place right on the lake, but the water pressure in the guest bathroom is an absolute joke. You turn on the shower and it's just this sad little trickle. Mark's dad keeps saying he's going to fix the pipes, but he's been saying that since we started dating five years ago. He's always starting these DIY projects and never finishing them. Like last Thanksgiving, he decided to build a birdhouse out of reclaimed wood from an old barn, and it's still just sitting in pieces on his workbench. But anyway, I was in the toothpaste aisle and I just had this sudden feeling, so I grabbed a test on a whim. And yeah, since right around then, it's just been this constant, heavy feeling every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, have you felt restless or been unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely have. Everything I do just feels so slowed down right now. It's honestly like I'm moving through syrup or something. Even doing really simple things at the clinic, like gathering wound care supplies or finishing up my charting, it's taking me noticeably longer than it should. Which is so frustrating because our charting system is already a nightmare. They updated the software last month to this new program called HealthSync, and it's supposed to be more \"streamlined,\" but it actually takes like five extra clicks just to enter a patient's temperature. And the IT guy, Kevin, came in to give us a tutorial on it, but he spent the first twenty minutes just complaining about his commute. He drives in from the suburbs and apparently there's this one intersection where the light takes a full four minutes to change. He actually timed it on his phone. He showed me the stopwatch app and everything. He has this really weird phone case, too, it looks like a giant silicone waffle. I asked him where he got it and he said his niece bought it for him at a kiosk in the mall. I haven't been to the mall in ages, mostly because the parking lot is a disaster since they started building that new movie theater. But yeah, I just feel really sluggish and slow doing everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt fatigued or noticed a loss of energy almost every day for the past two weeks, even when you haven't been physically active?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I haven't really noticed feeling physically tired or like I don't have energy. I mean, my energy levels are pretty much the same as always. I still drink my morning coffee, but that's just my normal routine. Though I might have to start stopping at a drive-thru before work because the Keurig in the clinic breakroom is completely ruined right now. Dr. Evans tried to descale it last week using straight white vinegar, and he definitely didn't run enough water through it afterward to rinse it out. The entire back hallway smelled exactly like a pickle factory for three days. It was so gross. And he always buys those really cheap off-brand pods from the bulk store, the ones where the foil lid is super flimsy? So they keep exploding, and now there's just crusted coffee grounds permanently jammed up in the little needle part. I actually tried to clean it out yesterday with a paperclip, but I slipped and poked my thumb. I had to go dig a Band-Aid out of the pediatric supply bin, so I've been walking around taking adult patients' vitals with a giant SpongeBob SquarePants on my hand. The kids love them, obviously, but Mr. Henderson—the tomato guy—he actually asked me if SpongeBob was a type of Swiss cheese. But yeah, um... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, fatigue. No, I wouldn't say I've been fatigued or lacking energy, I just feel really slow doing things, like I mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so restless inside that you couldn't sit still, or found yourself pacing, fidgeting, or moving around more than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt restless like that. I'm not really pacing or fidgeting or anything. Honestly, I spend most of my time sitting at the nurses' station when I'm not in with a patient. The chairs there are awful, by the way. They're those rolling office chairs from like, the 1990s, and the fabric on mine is this weird, scratchy maroon color. Someone spilled hand sanitizer on it last year and it left this giant bleached stain that looks exactly like a map of Florida. Which is funny, because my sister just moved to Florida. She got a job in Orlando, working in the HR department for this company that makes pool noodles. I didn't even know there was a whole corporate structure for pool noodles, but apparently there is. She says it's so hot there that she has to keep her steering wheel covered with a towel when she parks at the grocery store. She drives a Honda Civic, one of the older ones, and the AC is completely shot. She took it to a mechanic down there and he quoted her like, eight hundred dollars to fix it. So she's just been driving with the windows down, which messes up her hair. She has this really curly hair, kind of like our mom's, and the humidity just makes it poof out. But um, anyway, no, I haven't felt restless or anything like that. Two years is a really long time. I mean, I've had times where I felt down, but definitely not for years at a time. The longest I've ever felt kind of stuck in a rut was maybe a few months back when I was in nursing school. That was brutal. The clinical rotations were just completely exhausting. I had this one instructor, Mrs. Gable, and she was absolutely terrifying. She used to wear these really loud, clunky clogs, so you could always hear her coming down the hallway. It sounded like a horse trotting on linoleum. We used to call her the Clydesdale, but obviously never to her face. She was obsessed with making sure our uniform scrubs were perfectly ironed. Like, who irons scrubs? They're supposed to be wash-and-wear. I bought this cheap iron from a thrift store just to appease her, but it had this weird short in the cord, so if you wiggled it the wrong way, it would spark. I actually scorched a hole right through the pocket of my favorite top. I had to sew a patch over it, and I used this thread that was slightly the wrong shade of blue, and Mrs. Gable totally noticed. She gave me a whole lecture about professional appearance right in front of a patient who was eating jello. It was cherry jello, which is the worst flavor, in my opinion. But yeah, anyway, no, I haven't felt down or lost interest in things for two whole years. That sounds awful.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or lost interest in most things, more days than not, before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I've never really had a time like that before this started, definitely not for two weeks straight. I mean, I'm usually a pretty upbeat person. Like, even when things get crazy at the clinic. Just last week, we had this massive delivery of medical supplies, and the delivery guy—he's this new guy, really young, maybe early twenties—he stacked all the boxes right in front of the supply closet door. Like, completely blocked it. And of course, that was the exact moment Dr. Evans decided he urgently needed a specific type of tongue depressor. The flavored ones, you know? We have grape and cherry for the kids, but he specifically wanted the grape ones because he says they taste less like cough syrup. Anyway, I had to move like fifteen boxes of gauze and saline solution just to get the door open. And the boxes were so heavy! I don't even know why we ordered that much saline. We barely go through a fraction of that in a month. I think the office manager, Brenda, might have accidentally added an extra zero to the order form. She's been a little distracted lately because her daughter is getting married in the fall, and they're having all this drama over the floral arrangements. Apparently, hydrangeas are out of season or something? I don't know much about flowers, honestly. No, not even for a few days. I've always been pretty steady, so nothing like that has ever happened before. 

Speaking of episodes, though, I was watching an episode of this baking competition on TV last night, and they had to make these elaborate spun-sugar sculptures.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F32.2, F33.1, F33.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, well, I came in because for the past few weeks I’ve just felt this... this heavy, sad pressure sitting on me almost all day. It's like I can't shake it. And, you know, I work as an LPN, so I'm always having to put on a brave face. Just yesterday, I was in with Mr. Henderson—he's this sweet older man on my floor who always asks about my cat, Barnaby. Barnaby actually just had to get switched to this expensive prescription diet because he was throwing up on my rugs, which is a whole other nightmare trying to get him to actually eat the new kibble... but anyway, I'm there at work, smiling at Mr. Henderson and the other patients, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any loss of interest or pleasure in activities you usually enjoy, like spending time with your cat or anything else that normally brings you joy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. I mean, after my shifts, I used to always do something to decompress, you know? I would paint, or go to yoga, or even take salsa classes. I used to really love salsa. My friend Jenna dragged me to this studio downtown a couple years ago, and the instructor there was so intense, always clapping and yelling about our footwork. Jenna actually just twisted her ankle doing Zumba last week, so she's stuck in this massive walking boot, which is a total nightmare for her because she lives in a third-floor walk-up and her dog refuses to go down the stairs by himself... but anyway, even before she hurt her ankle, I had completely stopped going. \n\nLately I just go straight home, sit on the couch, and that's it. Jenna will text me, or my mom will text me, and I just ignore all of them. I look at the phone and just put it face down because none of it even feels worth the effort anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the sadness and loss of interest in activities?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been about three weeks now. It started right after I found out I was pregnant, which... you know, was a huge shock. I wasn't planning on it, and my boyfriend, Mark, he's currently out of town on this massive contracting job in Ohio. He builds these custom decks, like the really fancy ones with the built-in seating and the pergolas. He's been sending me pictures of the wood grain, which is nice, I guess, but it's just so loud on the construction site he can barely hear me when I call. And his truck just broke down last week, so he's been having to borrow his boss's van, which smells entirely like wet dog and stale French fries... but yeah, anyway, it's been about three weeks since I found out, and that's when all this really started feeling so heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately, like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than usual?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Um, no, not really. I mean, I think I'm sleeping okay. I usually go to bed around the same time, but honestly, the mattress we have is starting to get this weird dip on my side. Mark says it's because I always sleep curled up in the exact same spot, but I swear it's just a cheap mattress. We bought it online from one of those places that ships it to you in a tiny box, and you have to cut the plastic and it just... whoosh, inflates. It was terrifying, actually, I thought it was going to knock over my nightstand. My nightstand is this antique thing I found at a flea market, real heavy oak, but the bottom drawer always sticks if it gets too humid. And lately, the humidity has been awful, hasn't it? Just walking to my car after my shift, I feel like I'm swimming through the air. But anyway, yeah, I don't think my sleep has really changed much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately, or found yourself feeling excessively guilty or worthless?\n\nPatient: I haven't really been eating much, honestly. I know I should be eating regularly, especially now with the pregnancy and everything, but most days food just sounds completely unappealing. Like, I'll be halfway through a shift at the clinic and suddenly realize all I've had all day is some crackers and coffee. The coffee at the clinic is terrible, by the way. They buy these massive tubs of the cheapest grounds they can find, and the machine hasn't been descaled since I started working there. My coworker, Sarah, tried to bring in her own French press last month, but then she accidentally knocked it off the breakroom table and glass went everywhere. It took us like forty-five minutes to clean it up because our custodian, Gary, was on vacation in Florida. He always goes to this same spot in Boca Raton where his sister has a condo, and he brings back these really tacky seashell magnets for everyone. I have like three of them on my fridge right now, next to this picture of my cat... but yeah, I just don't really have an appetite. As for feeling guilty, I don't know, I just feel like I'm doing everything wrong right now.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, or have you experienced a lack of interest or pleasure in most activities most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... that's kind of why I made the appointment. For the past few weeks, I've just felt this heavy, sad pressure sitting on me almost all day. It's like I'm dragging around wet sand. And it's so hard because at the clinic, I have to be the cheerful nurse, you know? I'm taking blood pressures and smiling at the patients, asking about their grandchildren, but inside I feel like I'm barely holding myself together. One of my patients yesterday, Mr. Henderson, he brought in these fresh tomatoes from his garden. Huge, beautiful heirlooms. He's so sweet, he always wears these suspenders with little trout on them because he used to be a fly fisherman. He told me all about how you have to tie the flies yourself to really get the big ones, and he uses this special kind of feather... I can't remember what bird it's from, maybe a pheasant? Anyway, he was so proud of those tomatoes, and I just stood there smiling and thanking him, but all I wanted to do was go hide in the supply closet and cry. I ended up leaving the tomatoes on the breakroom counter because I knew I wouldn't eat them, and I think Sarah took them home to make salsa. She makes this really spicy mango salsa that she brings to our summer potlucks, but she always uses way too much cilantro. I have that gene, you know, where cilantro tastes exactly like dish soap? It completely ruins tacos for me. But yeah, nearly every day it's just been this overwhelming, hopeless feeling that I can't shake.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood or lack of interest in things has been present most of the day, nearly every day, for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really has. I'd say it's been going on for about three weeks now. It started shortly after I found out I was pregnant. Which, honestly, finding out was a whole thing in itself. I was at the drugstore buying those little travel-sized toothpastes because Mark and I were supposed to go up to his parents' cabin for the weekend. His parents have this gorgeous place right on the lake, but the water pressure in the guest bathroom is an absolute joke. You turn on the shower and it's just this sad little trickle. Mark's dad keeps saying he's going to fix the pipes, but he's been saying that since we started dating five years ago. He's always starting these DIY projects and never finishing them. Like last Thanksgiving, he decided to build a birdhouse out of reclaimed wood from an old barn, and it's still just sitting in pieces on his workbench. But anyway, I was in the toothpaste aisle and I just had this sudden feeling, so I grabbed a test on a whim. And yeah, since right around then, it's just been this constant, heavy feeling every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, have you felt restless or been unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely have. Everything I do just feels so slowed down right now. It's honestly like I'm moving through syrup or something. Even doing really simple things at the clinic, like gathering wound care supplies or finishing up my charting, it's taking me noticeably longer than it should. Which is so frustrating because our charting system is already a nightmare. They updated the software last month to this new program called HealthSync, and it's supposed to be more \"streamlined,\" but it actually takes like five extra clicks just to enter a patient's temperature. And the IT guy, Kevin, came in to give us a tutorial on it, but he spent the first twenty minutes just complaining about his commute. He drives in from the suburbs and apparently there's this one intersection where the light takes a full four minutes to change. He actually timed it on his phone. He showed me the stopwatch app and everything. He has this really weird phone case, too, it looks like a giant silicone waffle. I asked him where he got it and he said his niece bought it for him at a kiosk in the mall. I haven't been to the mall in ages, mostly because the parking lot is a disaster since they started building that new movie theater. But yeah, I just feel really sluggish and slow doing everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt fatigued or noticed a loss of energy almost every day for the past two weeks, even when you haven't been physically active?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I haven't really noticed feeling physically tired or like I don't have energy. I mean, my energy levels are pretty much the same as always. I still drink my morning coffee, but that's just my normal routine. Though I might have to start stopping at a drive-thru before work because the Keurig in the clinic breakroom is completely ruined right now. Dr. Evans tried to descale it last week using straight white vinegar, and he definitely didn't run enough water through it afterward to rinse it out. The entire back hallway smelled exactly like a pickle factory for three days. It was so gross. And he always buys those really cheap off-brand pods from the bulk store, the ones where the foil lid is super flimsy? So they keep exploding, and now there's just crusted coffee grounds permanently jammed up in the little needle part. I actually tried to clean it out yesterday with a paperclip, but I slipped and poked my thumb. I had to go dig a Band-Aid out of the pediatric supply bin, so I've been walking around taking adult patients' vitals with a giant SpongeBob SquarePants on my hand. The kids love them, obviously, but Mr. Henderson—the tomato guy—he actually asked me if SpongeBob was a type of Swiss cheese. But yeah, um... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, fatigue. No, I wouldn't say I've been fatigued or lacking energy, I just feel really slow doing things, like I mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so restless inside that you couldn't sit still, or found yourself pacing, fidgeting, or moving around more than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt restless like that. I'm not really pacing or fidgeting or anything. Honestly, I spend most of my time sitting at the nurses' station when I'm not in with a patient. The chairs there are awful, by the way. They're those rolling office chairs from like, the 1990s, and the fabric on mine is this weird, scratchy maroon color. Someone spilled hand sanitizer on it last year and it left this giant bleached stain that looks exactly like a map of Florida. Which is funny, because my sister just moved to Florida. She got a job in Orlando, working in the HR department for this company that makes pool noodles. I didn't even know there was a whole corporate structure for pool noodles, but apparently there is. She says it's so hot there that she has to keep her steering wheel covered with a towel when she parks at the grocery store. She drives a Honda Civic, one of the older ones, and the AC is completely shot. She took it to a mechanic down there and he quoted her like, eight hundred dollars to fix it. So she's just been driving with the windows down, which messes up her hair. She has this really curly hair, kind of like our mom's, and the humidity just makes it poof out. But um, anyway, no, I haven't felt restless or anything like that. Just really slow, like I said before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods in your life where you felt down or lost interest in things for most of the day, more days than not, for as long as two years?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I've ever felt like that for that long. Two years is a really long time. I mean, I've had times where I felt down, but definitely not for years at a time. The longest I've ever felt kind of stuck in a rut was maybe a few months back when I was in nursing school. That was brutal. The clinical rotations were just completely exhausting. I had this one instructor, Mrs. Gable, and she was absolutely terrifying. She used to wear these really loud, clunky clogs, so you could always hear her coming down the hallway. It sounded like a horse trotting on linoleum. We used to call her the Clydesdale, but obviously never to her face. She was obsessed with making sure our uniform scrubs were perfectly ironed. Like, who irons scrubs? They're supposed to be wash-and-wear. I bought this cheap iron from a thrift store just to appease her, but it had this weird short in the cord, so if you wiggled it the wrong way, it would spark. I actually scorched a hole right through the pocket of my favorite top. I had to sew a patch over it, and I used this thread that was slightly the wrong shade of blue, and Mrs. Gable totally noticed. She gave me a whole lecture about professional appearance right in front of a patient who was eating jello. It was cherry jello, which is the worst flavor, in my opinion. But yeah, anyway, no, I haven't felt down or lost interest in things for two whole years. That sounds awful.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or lost interest in most things, more days than not, before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I've never really had a time like that before this started, definitely not for two weeks straight. I mean, I'm usually a pretty upbeat person. Like, even when things get crazy at the clinic. Just last week, we had this massive delivery of medical supplies, and the delivery guy—he's this new guy, really young, maybe early twenties—he stacked all the boxes right in front of the supply closet door. Like, completely blocked it. And of course, that was the exact moment Dr. Evans decided he urgently needed a specific type of tongue depressor. The flavored ones, you know? We have grape and cherry for the kids, but he specifically wanted the grape ones because he says they taste less like cough syrup. Anyway, I had to move like fifteen boxes of gauze and saline solution just to get the door open. And the boxes were so heavy! I don't even know why we ordered that much saline. We barely go through a fraction of that in a month. I think the office manager, Brenda, might have accidentally added an extra zero to the order form. She's been a little distracted lately because her daughter is getting married in the fall, and they're having all this drama over the floral arrangements. Apparently, hydrangeas are out of season or something? I don't know much about flowers, honestly. I tried to keep a succulent alive on my desk once, but I think I overwatered it because it just turned to mush. It was really sad. But, um... yeah, to answer your question, no, I've never felt this way for a couple of weeks before all this current stuff started.\n\nDoctor: Before this current episode, have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or lost interest in most things, more days than not?\n\nPatient: Wait, didn't you just ask me that? Or maybe I'm just getting confused, sorry. But no, I haven't ever had anything like this happen before. This is the first time I've felt this way. \n\nHonestly, repeating things is basically my whole day at the clinic right now anyway. Like, our fax machine has been stuck in this weird loop since Monday. It keeps printing out the exact same lunch menu from this Thai restaurant down the street. We didn't even order from there! Though, honestly, I love Pad Thai. There's this place near my apartment that makes it perfectly spicy, but they always skimp on the crushed peanuts. I don't get why they're so stingy with the peanuts, they buy them in bulk. I actually bought a giant jar of dry-roasted peanuts from Costco just to keep in my pantry so I can add my own when I get takeout. I got the honey-roasted ones by mistake last time, which sounds kind of gross to put in noodles, but it actually wasn't terrible. It gave it this whole sweet and savory vibe. I tried to tell Brenda about it—the office manager I mentioned—but she was on the phone arguing with the caterer about whether or not they should serve mini quiches at the wedding reception. Mini quiches always get cold too fast, if you ask me. \n\nBut anyway, sorry, what were we saying? Oh, right. No, I've never had a two-week period of feeling down like this before. It's all totally new.\n\nDoctor: Before this current episode, have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or lost interest in most things, more days than not?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're going in circles, Doc. Like I said, no. This is definitely the first time I've ever felt this... this bad for this long. Two weeks, two years, whatever—it's never happened before. \n\nIt's funny you keep asking the same thing, though, because it reminds me of my cat, Barnaby. He's this massive orange tabby, and he has this routine every morning where he paws at the same spot on the bedroom door until I get up to feed him. It's not even a scratch, it's just this dull *thump, thump, thump*. And if I ignore him, he doesn't stop. He just waits exactly three minutes and does it again. I actually timed him once. I bought this little digital stopwatch because I thought it was so weird. The stopwatch was neon green, which I loved, but I accidentally left it in the pocket of my scrub pants and it went through the wash. Totally ruined it. Which is a shame, because I was going to use it to time how long it takes Dr. Evans to dictate his notes. He talks so slow, it's agonizing. He pauses between every other word like he's waiting for profound inspiration to strike. I swear, I could knit a whole sweater in the time it takes him to finish a single patient chart. I tried knitting once, actually, but I kept dropping stitches, so I ended up with this weird, lumpy triangle instead of a scarf. \n\nBut yeah, no. Never had this happen before. This is all completely new territory for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other episodes in your life where you felt persistently down or lost interest in things for most of the day, more days than not, even if they didn't last as long as this current one?\n\nPatient: No, not even for a few days. I've always been pretty steady, so nothing like that has ever happened before. \n\nSpeaking of episodes, though, I was watching an episode of this baking competition on TV last night, and they had to make these elaborate spun-sugar sculptures. This one lady's entire caramel dome just collapsed right at the last second. It was devastating! I felt so bad for her. I tried making caramel once for a flan, but I burned the sugar so badly it set off the smoke detector in my apartment. My landlord, Mr. Henderson, came running up the stairs in his bathrobe. He wears this really loud plaid bathrobe, like lumberjack style, even in the middle of summer. He's a nice guy, though. He fixed my garbage disposal last month when it got jammed on a lemon rind. I read online that putting lemon rinds in the disposal makes it smell fresh, but nobody tells you it can completely break the blades if it's an old unit. It cost him like two hundred dollars to replace it, and I felt terrible. I baked him some cookies to apologize, but I used store-bought dough because of the whole caramel incident. I just don't trust myself with melting sugar anymore. \n\nBut um, wait, what were you asking? Shorter times feeling down? No, definitely not. Never happened before this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
